.

B
Material in support of the proof of Theorem
4.1
: the required UCP and
\displaystyle D^{*}{\mathcal{B}}_{q}^{*}f
in (
4.18
).

B.1
The required UCP.

We return to the operator
\displaystyle\mathbb{A}_{q}
in (
1.34
):

\mathbb{A}_{q}=\begin{bmatrix}{\mathcal{A}}_{q}&-{\mathcal{C}}_{\gamma}\\
-{\mathcal{C}}_{\theta_{e}}&-{\mathcal{B}}_{q}\end{bmatrix}:\boldsymbol{W}^{q}_{\sigma}(\Omega)=\boldsymbol{L}^{q}_{\sigma}(\Omega)\times L^{q}(\Omega)\supset{\mathcal{D}}(\mathbb{A}_{q})={\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{A}}_{q})\times{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{B}}_{q})\\
=(\boldsymbol{W}^{2,q}(\Omega)\cap\boldsymbol{W}^{1,q}_{0}(\Omega)\cap\boldsymbol{L}^{q}_{\sigma}(\Omega))\times(W^{2,q}(\Omega)\cap W^{1,q}_{0}(\Omega))\longrightarrow\boldsymbol{W}^{q}_{\sigma}(\Omega).

(B.1)

With
\boldsymbol{\Phi}=[\boldsymbol{\varphi},\psi],
the PDE-version of
\mathbb{A}_{q}\boldsymbol{\Phi}=\lambda\boldsymbol{\Phi}
is

\displaystyle-\nu\Delta\boldsymbol{\varphi}+L_{e}(\boldsymbol{\varphi})+\nabla\pi-\gamma\psi\boldsymbol{e}_{d}

\displaystyle=\lambda\boldsymbol{\varphi}

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega

(B.2a)

\displaystyle-\kappa\Delta\psi-\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\cdot\nabla\psi+\boldsymbol{\varphi}\cdot\nabla\theta_{e}

\displaystyle=\lambda\psi

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega

(B.2b)

\displaystyle\text{div }\boldsymbol{\varphi}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega

(B.2c)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varphi}=0,\ \psi

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{on }\Gamma.

(B.2d)

Several UCP for over-determined versions of the eigenproblem (
B.2
) are given in
[
89
]
. However, establishing in Theorem
4.1
controllability of the finite dimensional projected problem (
4.23
) or (
4.29
) via verification of the Kalman rank condition (
4.31
) involves the following UCP for the adjoint problem. First, recall from (
4.4
) that the adjoint
\displaystyle\mathbb{A}_{q}^{*}
or
\mathbb{A}_{q}
in (
B.1
) is

\mathbb{A}_{q}^{*}=\begin{bmatrix}{\mathcal{A}}_{q}^{*}&-{\mathcal{C}}_{\theta_{e}}^{*}\\
-{\mathcal{C}}_{\gamma}^{*}&-{\mathcal{B}}_{q}^{*}\end{bmatrix}:\boldsymbol{W}^{q^{\prime}}_{\sigma}(\Omega)=\boldsymbol{L}^{q^{\prime}}_{\sigma}(\Omega)\times L^{q^{\prime}}(\Omega)\supset{\mathcal{D}}(\mathbb{A}_{q}^{*})={\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{A}}_{q}^{*})\times{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{B}}_{q}^{*})\\
=(\boldsymbol{W}^{2,q^{\prime}}(\Omega)\cap\boldsymbol{W}^{1,q^{\prime}}_{0}(\Omega)\cap\boldsymbol{L}^{q^{\prime}}_{\sigma}(\Omega))\times(W^{2,q^{\prime}}(\Omega)\cap\boldsymbol{W}^{1,q^{\prime}}_{0}(\Omega))\longrightarrow\boldsymbol{W}^{q^{\prime}}_{\sigma}(\Omega).

(B.3)

With
\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{*}=[\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{*},\psi^{*}],
the PDE-version of
\mathbb{A}_{q}^{*}\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{*}=\lambda\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{*}
is

\displaystyle-\nu\Delta\boldsymbol{\varphi^{*}}+L^{*}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\varphi^{*}})+\psi^{*}\nabla\theta_{e}+\nabla\pi

\displaystyle=\lambda\boldsymbol{\varphi^{*}}

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega

(B.4a)

\displaystyle-\kappa\Delta\psi^{*}-\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\cdot\nabla\psi^{*}-\gamma\boldsymbol{\varphi^{*}}\cdot\boldsymbol{e}_{d}

\displaystyle=\lambda\psi^{*}

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega

(B.4b)

\displaystyle\text{div }\boldsymbol{\varphi^{*}}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega

(B.4c)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varphi^{*}}=0,\ \psi^{*}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{on }\Gamma.

(B.4d)

The UCP invoked in the proof of Theorem
4.1
is

Theorem B.1
.

[
89
, Theorem 5]

Let
\displaystyle\{\boldsymbol{\varphi},\psi,\pi\}\in\left[\boldsymbol{W}^{2,q}(\Omega)\cap\boldsymbol{L}^{q}_{\sigma}(\Omega)\right]\times W^{2,q}(\Omega),\ \pi\in W^{1,q}(\Omega),
be a solution of the following dual problem

\displaystyle-\nu\Delta\boldsymbol{\varphi^{*}}+L^{*}_{e}(\boldsymbol{\varphi^{*}})+\psi^{*}\nabla\theta_{e}+\nabla\pi

\displaystyle=\lambda\boldsymbol{\varphi^{*}}

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega

(B.5a)

\displaystyle-\kappa\Delta\psi^{*}-\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\cdot\nabla\psi^{*}-\gamma\boldsymbol{\varphi^{*}}\cdot\boldsymbol{e}_{d}

\displaystyle=\lambda\psi^{*}

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega

(B.5b)

\displaystyle\text{div }\boldsymbol{\varphi^{*}}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega

(B.5c)

\displaystyle\left\{\varphi^{*(1)},\dots,\varphi^{*(d-1)}\right\}=0,\ \psi^{*}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{on }\omega

(B.5d)

with over-determination in (
B.5d
) Then

\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{*}=0,\ \psi^{*}=0,\ \pi=const\ \text{in }\Omega.\ \qed

(B.6)

Proof of
\displaystyle D^{*}{\mathcal{B}}^{*}f=-\kappa\frac{\partial f}{\partial\nu}\bigg|_{\Gamma},\ f\in{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{B}}_{q}^{*})
, in (
4.18
) essentially by Green’s formula.

1.

Let
1<q<\infty
and define

B_{o,q}h=\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\cdot h,\ L^{q}(\Omega)\supset{\mathcal{D}}(B_{o,q})=W^{1,q}_{0}(\Omega)\longrightarrow L^{q}(\Omega)

(B.7)

Then

B_{o,q}^{*}h=-\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\cdot f,\ L^{q^{\prime}}(\Omega)\supset{\mathcal{D}}(B_{o,q}^{*})=W^{1,q^{\prime}}_{0}(\Omega)\longrightarrow L^{q^{\prime}}(\Omega)

(B.8)

In fact recalling
\boldsymbol{y}_{e}|_{\Gamma}\equiv 0
and div
\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\equiv 0
in
\Omega
from (
1.2c-d
), we compute in the duality pairing
L^{q}(\Omega),\ L^{q^{\prime}}(\Omega)
, with
h\in{\mathcal{D}}(B_{o,q}),\ f\in{\mathcal{D}}(B^{*}_{o,q})
by (
B.7
):

\displaystyle\left\langle B_{o,q}h,f\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}f\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\cdot\nabla hd\Omega=\int_{\Gamma}\cancel{hf\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\cdot\nu d\Gamma}-\int_{\Omega}h\text{ div}(f\boldsymbol{y}_{e})d\Omega

\displaystyle=-\int_{\Omega}h\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\cdot\nabla fd\Omega=\left\langle h,B^{*}_{o,q}f\right\rangle_{\Omega}

(B.9)

2.

We return to the operator
{\mathcal{B}}_{q}
in (
1.18
), with
h\in{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{B}}_{q})

{\mathcal{B}}_{q}h=-\kappa\Delta h+B_{o,q}h,\ \text{so that }{\mathcal{B}}_{q}^{*}h=-\kappa\Delta f+B_{o,q}^{*}f,\ f\in{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{B}}_{q}^{*})

(B.10)

We shall show that

\displaystyle D^{*}{\mathcal{B}}^{*}f=-\kappa\frac{\partial f}{\partial\nu}\bigg|_{\Gamma},\ f\in{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{B}}_{q}^{*})=W^{2,q^{\prime}}(\Omega)\cap W^{1,q^{\prime}}_{0}(\Omega)

(B.11)

In fact, by (
B.8
) and (
B.10
) we compute with
v\in L^{2}(\Gamma)
recalling the definition of
D
in (
1.23a
)

\displaystyle\left\langle D^{*}{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{q}f,v\right\rangle_{\Gamma}

\displaystyle=\left\langle{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{q}f,Dv\right\rangle_{\Omega}=\left\langle\Delta f,-\kappa Dv\right\rangle_{\Omega}+\left\langle f,B^{*}_{o,q}Dv\right\rangle_{\Omega}

(B.12)

\displaystyle=\left\langle f,\cancel{(-\kappa\Delta-\boldsymbol{y}_{e}\cdot\nabla)}Dv\right\rangle_{\Omega}+\int_{\Gamma}\frac{\partial f}{\partial\nu}(-\kappa Dv)d\Gamma+\int_{\Gamma}\cancel{f\left(\kappa\frac{\partial Dv}{\partial\nu}\right)}d\Gamma

(B.13)

\displaystyle=\left\langle-\kappa\frac{\partial f}{\partial\nu},v\right\rangle_{\Gamma},\quad\text{for all }v\in L^{q}(\Gamma).

(B.14)

and (
B.11
) is established.
∎

C
Validation of the Kalman controllability conditions (
4.31
) with fluid vectors
\displaystyle\{\boldsymbol{u}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{u}_{\ell_{i}}\},\ \ell_{i}\leq K,\ i=1,\dots,M
having only
(d-1)
components.

For the sake of the clarity, we shall consider separately the cases
d=2
and
d=3
.

Case
d=2
.
Express the
2
-dimensional vectors
\boldsymbol{u}_{i}
and
\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{ij}^{*}
of Section
4
in terms of their two components.

\boldsymbol{u}_{i}=\left[u^{(1)}_{i},u^{(2)}_{i}\right],\ \boldsymbol{\varphi}^{*}_{ij}=\left[\varphi_{ij}^{*(1)},\varphi_{ij}^{*(2)}\right],\ i=1,\dots,M,\ j=1,\dots,\ell_{i}

(C.1)

Here we shall recall the matrix
\boldsymbol{W}_{i}
in (
4.24
) but we shall replace the matrix
U_{i}
in (
4.25
) with the following matrix
U_{i}^{(2)}
:

U^{(2)}_{i}=\begin{bmatrix}\left\langle u^{(2)}_{1},\varphi_{i1}^{*(2)}\right\rangle_{\omega}&\dots&\left\langle u^{(2)}_{\ell_{i}},\varphi_{i1}^{*(2)}\right\rangle_{\omega}\\[5.69054pt]
\left\langle u^{(2)}_{2},\varphi_{i2}^{*(2)}\right\rangle_{\omega}&\dots&\left\langle u^{(2)}_{\ell_{i}},\varphi_{i2}^{*(2)}\right\rangle_{\omega}\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
\left\langle u^{(2)}_{1},\varphi_{i\ell_{i}}^{*(2)}\right\rangle_{\omega}&\dots&\left\langle u^{(2)}_{\ell_{i}},\varphi_{i\ell_{i}}^{*(2)}\right\rangle_{\omega}\end{bmatrix}:\ell_{i}\times K

(C.2)

where now the duality pairing
\left\langle\ ,\ \right\rangle_{\omega}
involves two scalar functions. Next, for
d=2
, we shall establish the Kalman algebraic rank condition for the finite dimensional, unstable, feedback problem (
4.23
), by employing not the full strength of the
2
-dimensional vectors
\boldsymbol{u}_{i}
and
\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{ij}^{*}
as in (
4.31
) involving the matrix
U_{i}
in (
4.25
), but instead replacing
U_{i}
with
U_{i}^{(2)}
. This way, only the second (scalar) components
u^{(2)}_{i}
and
\varphi_{ij}^{*(2)}
of the
2
-dimensional vectors
\boldsymbol{u}_{i}
and
\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{ij}^{*}
in (
C.1
) are needed. Recall that we are dealing with a pair
\{\omega,\widetilde{\Gamma}\}
as in Fig 1. The counterpart of Theorem
4.1
is now

Theorem C.1
.

Let
d=2
. It is possible to select boundary vectors
f_{1},\dots,f_{K}
in
{\mathcal{F}}\subset W^{2-{}^{1}\!/_{q},q}(\widetilde{\Gamma})
with support on
\widetilde{\Gamma}
, and scalar second components
\{u^{(2)}_{1},\dots,u^{(2)}_{\ell_{i}}\}
as in (
C.1
), such that

\text{rank }\left[W_{i},\ U_{i}^{(2)}\right]=\ell_{i},\ i,\dots,M.

(C.3)

Proof.

We shall appropriately modify the proof of Theorem
4.1
. As in this proof, the crux is to establish that we cannot have simultaneously

\partial_{\nu}\psi_{i\ell_{i}}^{*}=\sum_{j=1}^{\ell_{i}-1}\alpha_{j}\partial_{\nu}\psi_{ij}^{*}\text{ in }L^{q}(\widetilde{\Gamma})\text{ and }\varphi^{*(2)}_{i\ell_{i}}=\sum_{j=1}^{\ell_{i}-1}\alpha_{j}\varphi^{*(2)}_{ij}\text{ in }L^{q}_{\sigma}(\omega)

(C.4)

with the same constant
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{\ell_{i}-1}
in both expression.

Claim: Statement (
C.4
) is false.
By contradiction, suppose that both linear combinations in (
C.4
) hold true. Next, as in (
4.34
) define the
(d+1)
-vector
\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{*}\equiv\{\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{*},\psi^{*}\}
by

\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{*}\equiv\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{*}\\
\psi^{*}\end{bmatrix}=\sum_{j=1}^{\ell_{i}-1}\begin{bmatrix}\alpha_{j}\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{ij}^{*}\\
\alpha_{j}\psi_{ij}^{*}\end{bmatrix}-\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{i\ell_{i}}^{*}\\
\psi_{i\ell_{i}}^{*}\end{bmatrix}=\sum_{j=1}^{\ell_{i}-1}\alpha_{j}\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{*}_{ij}-\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{*}_{i\ell_{i}},\ i=1,\dots,M;\ q\geq 2,

(C.5)

in
\boldsymbol{W}^{q}_{\sigma}(\Omega)\equiv\boldsymbol{L}^{q}_{\sigma}(\Omega)\times L^{q}(\Omega)
, with
\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{*}_{ij}\equiv\{\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{*}_{ij},\psi^{*}_{ij}\}
eigenvector of
\displaystyle\mathbb{A}_{q,N}^{*}
or
\displaystyle\mathbb{A}_{q}^{*}
, as in (
4.3
). Then, in view of (
C.4
), we now obtain

\varphi^{*(2)}\equiv 0\text{ in }\omega,\quad\partial_{\nu}\psi^{*}|_{\widetilde{\Gamma}}\equiv 0.

(C.6)

(in place of (
4.35
)). As in the proof of Theorem
4.1
, since the
\displaystyle\Phi^{*}_{ij}
are eigenvectors of
\displaystyle\mathbb{A}_{q,N}^{*}
, or
\displaystyle\mathbb{A}_{q}^{*}
, so is the vector
\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{*}\equiv\{\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{*},\psi^{*}\}
defined in (
C.5
). Thus
\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{*}\equiv\{\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{*},\psi^{*}\}
satisfies the PDE version (
4.36a-d
) of the eigenproblem for the dual
\displaystyle\mathbb{A}_{q}^{*}
, which is now augmented with the over-determined conditions in (
C.6
). The fact that now
\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{*}|_{\Gamma}=0
by (
4.36d
) on the entire boundary
\Gamma
, permits a-fortiori the argument of
[
89
, Theorems 6, 7]
to hold true. Namely, invoke
\displaystyle\varphi^{*(2)}\equiv 0
in
\omega
in (
C.6
) to obtain, as in the proof of Theorem
4.1
, the over-determined problem (
4.37
) for
\psi^{*}
on
\omega
, with the conclusion that

\psi^{*}\equiv 0\text{ in }\omega,\text{ as in }\eqref{4.38}

(C.7)

Next, the divergence condition (
4.36c
) yields in view of (
C.6
):
div\ \boldsymbol{\varphi}^{*}=\varphi^{*(1)}_{x_{1}}+\varphi^{*(2)}_{x_{2}}=\varphi^{*(1)}_{x_{1}}=0
in
\omega
. Hence
\varphi^{*(1)}(x_{1},x_{2})\equiv c(x_{2})
in
\omega
, where
c(x_{2})
is a function constant w.r.t.
x_{1}
and depending only on
x_{2}
in
\omega
. Next, let
\displaystyle P=\{x_{1}(P),x_{2}(P)\}
be an arbitrary point of
\omega
. Consider the line
\ell
passing through the point
P
and parallel to the
x_{1}
-axis. On such a line
\ell
, the value
\displaystyle\varphi^{*(1)}(x_{1},x_{2}(P))\equiv c_{2}(x_{2}(P))
is constant w.r.t.
x_{1}
, as long as
\ell
intersect
\omega
. By definition of the small set
\omega
supported by
\widetilde{\Gamma}
, there is a non-empty open subset
\widetilde{\omega}\subset\omega
, where this happens: for all points
P
in
\widetilde{\omega}
, the line
\ell
remains in
\widetilde{\omega}
and hits the boundary
\widetilde{\Gamma}
, where condition (
4.36d
) applies for
\displaystyle\varphi^{*(1)}|_{\Gamma}=0
. Thus
\varphi^{*(1)}\equiv 0
in
\widetilde{\omega}\subset\omega
. Recalling (
C.6
), we finally have

\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{*}=\{\varphi^{*(1)},\varphi^{*(2)}\}\equiv 0\text{ in }\widetilde{\omega}\subset\omega,\text{ along with }\psi^{*}\equiv 0\text{ in }\widetilde{\omega}\text{ by }\eqref{C.7}.

(C.8)

We can then apply
[
89
, Theorem 5 for
\widetilde{\omega}
]
and conclude that

\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{*}=\{\varphi^{*(1)},\varphi^{*(2)}\}\equiv 0\text{ in }\Omega,\ \psi^{*}\equiv 0\text{ in }\Omega,\ p\equiv\text{ const in }\Omega.

(C.9)

The rest of the proof proceeds as in Theorem
4.1
following (
4.40
). Theorem
C.1
is established.
∎

\dots&\dots&\dots\\
\left\langle\begin{bmatrix}u^{(2)}_{1}\\[2.84526pt]
u^{(3)}_{1}\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}\varphi_{i\ell_{i}}^{*(2)}\\[2.84526pt]
\varphi_{i\ell_{i}}^{*(3)}\end{bmatrix}\right\rangle_{\omega}&\dots&\left\langle\begin{bmatrix}u^{(2)}_{\ell_{i}}\\[2.84526pt]
u^{(3)}_{\ell_{i}}\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}\varphi_{i\ell_{i}}^{*(2)}\\[2.84526pt]
\varphi_{i\ell_{i}}^{*(3)}\end{bmatrix}\right\rangle_{\omega}\end{bmatrix}

(C.12)

Next, for
d=3
, we shall establish the Kalman algebraic rank condition for the finite dimensional, unstable, feedback problem (
4.23
), by replacing the matrix
U_{i}
in (
4.25
), with either the matrix
U^{(1,3)}_{i}
in (
C
) (Sub-case
\{1,3\}
); or else with the matrix
U^{2,3}_{i}
in (
C
) (Sub-case
\{2,3\}
). This way, the third (scalar) components
u^{(3)}_{i}
and
\varphi^{*(3)}_{ij}
of the
3
-dimensional vectors
\boldsymbol{u}_{i}
and
\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{*}_{ij}
in (
C.10
) are needed in both sub-cases. The counterpart of Theorem
4.1
(or Theorem
C.1
) is now

Theorem C.2
.

Let
d=3
. It is possible to select boundary vectors
f_{1},\dots,f_{K}
in
{\mathcal{F}}\subset W^{2-{}^{1}\!/_{q},q}(\widetilde{\Gamma})
with support on
\widetilde{\Gamma}
, and

(i)

either
2
-dimensional vectors
\boldsymbol{u}^{(1,3)}_{i}=\left\{u^{(1)}_{i},u^{(3)}_{i}\right\}
as in (
C
)

(ii)

or else
2
-dimensional vectors
\boldsymbol{u}^{(2,3)}_{i}=\left\{u^{(2)}_{i},u^{(3)}_{i}\right\}
as in (
C
),

such that

\text{rank }\left[W_{i},\ U_{i}^{(1,3)}\right]=\ell_{i},\ i,\dots,M,\text{ in case }(i)

(C.13)

or

\text{rank }\left[W_{i},\ U_{i}^{(2,3)}\right]=\ell_{i},\ i,\dots,M,\text{ in case }(ii)

(C.14)

respectively.

Proof.

We shall only give a proof in the sub-case
\{1,3\}
, as the proof of the sub-case
\{2,3\}
is the same
mutatis mutandis
. As in the proof of Theorem
4.1
(or Theorem
C.1
), the crux is to establish that we cannot have simultaneously

\partial_{\nu}\psi_{i\ell_{i}}^{*}=\sum_{j=1}^{\ell_{i}-1}\alpha_{j}\partial_{\nu}\psi_{ij}^{*}\text{ in }L^{q}(\widetilde{\Gamma})\quad\text{and}\quad\varphi^{*(1,3)}_{i\ell_{i}}=\sum_{j=1}^{\ell_{i}-1}\alpha_{j}\varphi^{*(1,3)}_{ij}\text{ in }\boldsymbol{L}^{q}_{\sigma}(\omega)

(C.15)

with the same constants
\alpha,\dots,\alpha_{\ell_{i}-1}
in both expressions.
∎

Claim: Statement (
C.15
) is false.
By contradiction, suppose that both linear combinations in (
C.15
) hold true. Next, define the
(d+1)
-vector
\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{*}\equiv\{\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{*},\psi^{*}\}
as in (
4.34
) (or (
C.5
)). Then, in view of (
C.15
), we now obtain

\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{*(1,3)}\equiv\left\{\varphi^{*(1)},\varphi^{*(3)}\right\}\equiv 0\text{ in }\omega,\quad\partial_{\nu}\psi^{*}|_{\widetilde{\Gamma}}\equiv 0.

(C.16)

(in place of (
4.35
) or (
C.6
)). As in Theorem
4.1
(or Theorem
C.1
) the
\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{*}\equiv\{\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{*},\psi^{*}\}
is an eigenvector of
\mathbb{A}_{q}^{*}
and thus satisfies the corresponding PDE-version - that is problem (
4.36a-d
), augmented this time with the over-determined conditions in (
C.16
). Next, invoke
\varphi^{*(3)}\equiv 0
in
\omega
from (
C.16
) to obtain, as this the proof of Theorem
4.1
, the over-determined problem (
4.37
) for
\psi^{*}
in
\omega
, with the conclusion that

\psi^{*}\equiv 0\text{ in }\omega,

(C.17)

as in (
4.36
) (or (
C.7
)). Next, the divergence condition (
4.36c
) yields in the view of (
C.16
):
div\ \boldsymbol{\varphi}^{*}=\varphi^{*(1)}_{x_{1}}+\varphi^{*(2)}_{x_{2}}+\varphi^{*(3)}_{x_{3}}=\varphi^{*(2)}_{x_{2}}=0
in
\omega
. Hence
\varphi^{*(2)}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\equiv c(x_{1},x_{3})
in
\omega
, where
c(x_{1},x_{3})
is a function constant w.r.t.
x_{2}
and depending only on
x_{1}
and
x_{3}
in
\omega
. Next, let
\displaystyle P=\{x_{1}(P),x_{2}(P),x_{3}(P)\}
be an arbitrary point of
\omega
. Consider the plane
\pi_{P}
passing through the point
P
and parallel to the
\{x_{1},x_{3}\}
-coordinate plane. As the point
\{x_{1},x_{2}(P),x_{3}\}
of
\omega
runs over the plane
\pi_{P}
, the value
\displaystyle\varphi^{*(2)}(x_{1},x_{2}(P),x_{3})=c(x_{1},x_{3})
is independent of
x_{2}(P)
, as long as such plane
\pi_{P}
intersects
\omega
. By definition of the small
\omega
supported by
\widetilde{\Gamma}
, there is a non-empty open subset
\widetilde{\omega}\subset\omega
, where this happens: for all points
P
in
\widetilde{\omega}
, the plane
\pi_{P}
remains in
\widetilde{\omega}
and hits the boundary
\widetilde{\Gamma}
, where condition (
4.36d
) applies for
\displaystyle\varphi^{*(2)}|_{\Gamma}=0
. Thus
\displaystyle\varphi^{*(2)}\equiv 0
in
\widetilde{\omega}\subset\omega
. Recalling (
C.16
) we conclude that

\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{*}=\left\{\varphi^{*(1)},\varphi^{*(2)},\varphi^{*(3)}\right\}\equiv 0\text{ in }\widetilde{\omega}\subset\omega,\text{ along with }\psi^{*}\equiv 0\text{ in }\widetilde{\omega}\text{ by }\eqref{C.17}.

(C.18)

We can then apply
[
89
, Theorem 5 for
\widetilde{\omega}
]
and conclude that

\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{*}=\left\{\varphi^{*(1)},\varphi^{*(2)},\varphi^{*(3)}\right\}\equiv 0\text{ in }\Omega,\ \psi^{*}\equiv 0\text{ in }\Omega,\ p\equiv\text{ const in }\Omega.

(C.19)

The rest of the proof proceeds as in Theorem
4.1
following (
4.40
). Theorem
C.2
is established.
